 CED Phase-1: Preliminary Experiment Design

  Gregory Siemers, Nicholas Thompson, and Jesson Hutchinson

                                             LA-UR-25-23659
                                                July 1, 2025

Executive Summary
IER-516, Zirconium Test Assembly (ZTA), is a campaign to design, execute, and document a series
of high-fidelity critical benchmark experiments to validate current and future zirconium (Zr) and
zirconium hydride (ZrHx ) nuclear data evaluations. ZTA is a collaborative project between Los
Alamos National Laboratory and the French Autoritè de Sûretè Nuclèaire et de Radioprotection.
The experiments will be fueled with highly enriched uranium (HEU), and utilize the Comet crit-
ical assembly machine at the National Criticality Experiments Research Center. A total of ten
preliminary critical experiments were designed and optimized for Zr and ZrHx nuclear data sen-
sitivities using the MCNP-Particle Swarm Optimization methodology in the thermal, epithermal,
intermediate, and fast neutron energy regions.
 Of the ten experiments designed six include only Zr metal. In all but the thermal configuration,
which preferred high-density polyethylene (HDPE), a large Zr metal neutron reflector, analogous to
the Zeus copper reflector, was required to achieve maximum sensitivity to Zr nuclear data. HDPE
was found to be the optimal neutron moderator material for both the thermal and epithermal
experiments. Similarly, beryllium metal is the optimal neutron moderator material for the two
intermediate configurations. The final two configurations are unmoderated fast neutron systems;
one includes the aforementioned Zr reflector to maximize Zr cross section sensitivity while the
other is unreflected, which enables maximal sensitivity to Zr elastic scattering angular distributions.
Measurement and analysis techniques developed in the EUCLID experiments will be used to resolve
compensating errors that have been observed in evaluated Zr nuclear data.
 Four experiments, incorporating two distinct experimental configurations, were designed to val-
idate ZrHx thermal neutron scattering law (TSL) evaluations. The first experiment is fueled with
the high HEU mass Jemima plates while the second by the low HEU mass Class Foils. Differ-
ences in neutron reflection and U:H atomic ratios of these experiments propagate to differences
in neutron spectra and ZrHx sensitivities, enabling robust validation. Two experiments, one with
delta(δ)-phase ZrH1.6−1.7 and the other with epsilon(ϵ)-phase ZrH1.9−2.0 , are recommended on each
experiment platform to validate the new (δ)-phase and epsilon(ϵ)-phase ZrHx TSL evaluations in-
cluded in the ENDF/B-VIII.1 nuclear data library. Zr and ZrHx nuclear data sensitivities enabled
by all ten configurations included in the preliminary experiment design largely exceed those of
current benchmark experiments.

1 Introduction
Evaluated nuclear data are a requirement for simulating the transport of neutrons through matter.
A lack of available differential nuclear data and/or deficiencies in the formats used to represent
these data can lead to errors when simulating the behavior of a nuclear system. Zirconium (Zr) has
been identified by the US-DOE Nuclear Criticality Safety Program (NCSP), Los Alamos National
Laboratory (LANL), and the French Autoritè de Sûretè Nuclèaire et de Radioprotection (ASNR) as
an essential material to nuclear criticality safety and reactor technology. Due to its high importance,
NCSP recognized the need to improve Zr nuclear data, and subsequently provided funding for
new Zr differential cross section measurements and corresponding evaluations [1]. In addition to
sparse differential cross section and angular distribution data, Zr lacks highly sensitive benchmark
experiments, particularly in the intermediate energy region, to validate these new differential and
evaluated data. Nuclear criticality experiments provide robust validation of these nuclear data
since these experiments possess both high sensitivities to the nuclear data of interest and a low
uncertainty on the measured quantity. The detailed uncertainty quantification associated with
these experiments, likewise, enables accurate determination of evaluated nuclear data uncertainties
and covariance. Validation of zirconium hydride (ZrHx ) thermal scattering law (TSL) nuclear
data is also important for criticality safety purposes as ZrHx , and other metal hydrides, are under
consideration to be used in micro reactors [2]. This document provides a detailed justification
to curate, execute, and document Zr and ZrHx critical benchmark experiments at the National
Criticality Experiments Research Center (NCERC) as part of integral experiment request (IER)-
516 Zirconium Test Assembly (ZTA). As well as a suite of new critical experiment configurations
to validate both existing and future Zr differential nuclear data and evaluations, in all applicable
neutron energy regions.

1.1 Zirconium Nuclear Applications and Properties
Zirconium metal is present in significant quantities in nuclear facilities which span the entire low-
enriched uranium (LEU) fuel cycle; those most important for nuclear criticality safety are fuel
manufacturing and processing facilities as well as transportation. Zr is the primary constitute of
zirconium based alloys (zircaloys), which are favorable materials for nuclear applications due to
the low thermal neutron absorption cross section, high strength, and high corrosion resistance of
elemental Zr. Zircaloys, most commonly Zircaloy-2 and Zircaloy-4, have been extensively developed
[3], and are currently the nominal LEU fuel rod cladding material in both commercial boiling water
and pressurized water nuclear reactors. Other structural components of LEU fuel rods, such as plugs
and springs, are also commonly constructed with either zircaloys or Zr-Nb alloys [4]. Substantial
quantities of Zr are, therefore, also present in spent nuclear fuel. Lastly, zircaloys have been explored
as alternative neutron reflectors in thermal power reactors to further exploit its favorable neutronic
properties [5]. The typical compositions, in weight percent, of common zircaloys are reported in
Table 1-1. Zr is also a common impurity in hafnium (Hf), which is a strong neutron absorber used
in many nuclear systems.
 Elemental zirconium is comprised of five stable isotopes, each possessing unique nuclear prop-
erties. The natural abundance of each stable isotope of Zr, along with its resonance integral and
thermal neutron capture cross section, taken from the ENDF/B-VIII.1 (ENDF-8.1) nuclear data
library [6], are provided in Table 1-2. Zirconium is an interesting element to the nuclear physicist or
engineer as it sits nearly atop the p-wave strength function [7], and possess a neutron magic isotope,

90 Zr at N=50, which is rather reluctant to decay due to this nucleon shell closure [8]. These facets

give rise to some of the distinct characteristics of the stable Zr isotopes, such as low absorption
cross sections, low level densities, and the high nuclear reaction threshold energies. Some of which,
such as the 12.1 MeV 90 Zr(n,2n)89 Zr threshold reaction, are used extensively in both dosimetry [9]
and neutron flux characterization applications. For example, Zr activation foils were utilized during
the IER 153: Prompt Fission 235 Uranium Neutron Spectrum (PFUNS) irradiations [10]; where the
90 Zr(n,2n)89 Zr reaction possessed the second highest reaction threshold energy in the foil load out.

This reaction provided 235 U prompt fission neutron spectrum (PFNS) data above 12 MeV, which
had not been previously measured.

1.2 Zirconium Hydride
Transition metal hydrides, particularly ZrHx and yttrium hydride (YHx ), are at the forefront of
research efforts regarding advanced nuclear fuel matrices in new microreactors [2][13]. Therefore,
validation of ZrHx neutron interaction data is required for nuclear criticality safety as ZrHx is present
in significant quantities in both nuclear research and processing facilities. The high hydrogen density
of metal hydrides (Table 1-3) make these materials effective neutron moderators and reflectors,
allowing for smaller critical nuclear systems to be constructed. Furthermore, ZrHx and YHx retain
high hydrogen densities until temperatures of approximately 800°C and 1200°C, respectively, where
other common neutron moderator materials such as water or polyethylene are rendered ineffective
[12]. The low thermal neutron capture cross section of Zr makes ZrHx in particular an even more
effective neutron moderator. Metal hydrides are produced by diffusing hydrogen gas into a metal
stock under high temperatures and pressures, often referred to as hydriding [14]. The resultant

 Introduction

 (a) (b)

 Figure 1-1: (a) Phase diagram of the Zirconium-Hydrogen system [18]. (b) U-Zr-Hx TRIGA Nuclear Fuel [19].

chemical structure of a particular metal hydride is dependent on the aforementioned hydriding
conditions, and the final hydrogen concentration with the crystal lattice of the stock material [15].
A phase diagram of ZrHx is provided in Figure 1-1a. Delta (δ) and epsilon (ϵ) phase ZrHx are of
particular interest in nuclear applications since these phases of ZrHx possess the highest content
of hydrogen. In this discussion, the nominal H:Zr atomic ratios in these phases of ZrHx are 1.6
and 2, respectively. These forms of ZrHx were first implemented in a nuclear application as part of
the System for Nuclear Auxiliary Power (SNAP) space reactor program [16]. Uranium-Zirconium-
Hydride fuels are also currently deployed in TRIGA research reactors worldwide (Figure 1-1b) [17].

Los Alamos National Laboratory Page 1-3

2 Motivation
The primary motivators of this experimental campaign are listed below. Each will be discussed in
this section.

 1. To perform low-uncertainty critical benchmark experiments optimized for sensitivity to Zr
 nuclear data in neutron energy regions where no validation data exists.

 2. To provide validation of new differential Zr nuclear data measurements and evaluations for
 criticality safety applications.

 3. To resolve conflicts and compensating errors between evaluated Zr nuclear datasets, particu-
 larly, those of the ENDF/B-VIII.1, JEFF-3.3, and JENDL-5.0 nuclear data libraries.

 4. To validate thermal neutron scattering law evaluations of δ-phase ZrHx and ϵ-phase ZrHx ,
 particularly those included in the ENDF/B-VIII.1 library.

2.1 Existing Integral Benchmarks
Integral nuclear data experiments form the cornerstone of validating evaluated nuclear data. In
particular, the well-characterized and documented experiments which have been evaluated and ap-
proved for inclusion in the International Criticality Safety Benchmark Evaluation Project (ICSBEP)
handbook [20], or benchmark experiments, are most frequently considered for during the validation

  Figure 2-1: Unique ICSBEP benchmark experiments with sensitivity to elemental and isotopic Zr nuclear data compared to
those with sensitivity to 56 Fe. A score in an energy bin on this plot corresponds to a total cross section sensitivity coefficient of
 at least 1e-3.

Los Alamos National Laboratory Page 2-1

process. Although the ICSBEP contains nearly 5,500 benchmark experiments, few possess apprecia-
ble sensitivity to Zr nuclear data, and none were designed to maximize sensitivity to Zr nuclear data.
Figure 2-1 illustrates the lack of ICSBEP benchmark experiments with high sensitivity to Zr nuclear
data in all energy regions, especially the intermediate energy region. Benchmark experiments sensi-
tive to 56 Fe nuclear data are also provided to highlight the lack of benchmark experiments sensitive
to Zr when compared to other essential structural materials in nuclear systems.

2.2 Conflicts in Evaluated Zirconium Nuclear Data
From the inception of the ENDF/B nuclear data library (1960s) until the release of ENDF/B-VII.0
in 2006, only elemental evaluated Zr nuclear data was available. For the release of the ENDF/B-
VII.0 nuclear data library, new evaluations were performed on each stable isotope of Zr. These
evaluations were updated for ENDF/B-VII.1 in 2011, but have remained nearly unchanged through
the most modern release of the library, ENDF/B-VIII.1 in 2024. NCSP recognized the need to
update the Zr evaluations in ENDF/B-VIII.1 due to the high importance of Zr to nuclear criticality
safety applications. Thus, new evaluations of the stable Zr isotopes have commenced, incorporating
both new differential data and modeling capabilities. Specifically, the resolved resonance regions
(RRR) will now be described with the rigorous R-matrix resonance formalism rather than multi-level
Briet-Wigner, new unresolved resonances regions (URR) consistent with RRR will be implemented,
and the EMPIRE-based high energy region evaluation will leverage a new soft-rotational optical
potential recently developed for Zr [21]. The ability of the ENDF/B-VIII.0 (ENDF-8.0) (same Zr

 Figure 2-3: (a) 90 Zr integral elastic neutron scattering cross sections (b) 90 Zr elastic neutron scattering cross sections at a

data as ENDF-8.1), JEFF-3.3 [22], and JENDL-5.0 [23] Zr evaluations to reproduce the benchmark
keff values of ICSBEP benchmarks most sensitive to Zr cross sections is shown Figure 2-2. In these
calculations, only the Zr nuclear data was varied to demonstrate the propagation of Zr evaluation
differences to the resultant benchmark keff values. The largest spread in keff observed (approximately
600 pcm) indicates that large conflicts exist between the evaluated Zr nuclear datasets, especially
considering the relatively low Zr sensitivities of these systems.
 The ENDF-8.0 evaluation is observed to have a negative reactivity bias when compared to
the JEFF-3.3 and JENDL-5.0 Zr evaluations, surpassing the benchmark uncertainty in several
cases. Moreover, the ENDF-8.0 evaluation fails to reproduce LCT074 case 5 (-259 pcm), one of the
most modern and well-characterized benchmarks available; which consists of two LEU fuel lattices
separated by a 10 cm Zircaloy-2 slab submerged in water. The deviation between C/E values
computed with these Zr evaluations is primarily driven by conflicting elastic scattering angular
distributions (ESADs), elastic and inelastic scattering cross sections above 100 keV, and thermal
neutron radiative capture cross sections, in that order [21]. The differences in the differential and
double-differential Zr elastic scattering cross sections present between the ENDF-8.0, JEFF-3.3,
and JENDL-5.0 are shown in Figure 2-3. Although these differences may appear relatively small
in magnitude, propagating these specific discrepancies through benchmark eigenvalue calculations
reveals contributions on the order of hundreds of pcm. For example, the differences observed in
elastic scatting cross section between the JEFF-3.3 and ENDF-8.0 evaluations in the fission neutron
energy region (100 keV - 5 MeV) introduce biases of approximately +130 pcm and +120 pcm in
the UCT001 (case 2) and ICT003 (case 2) benchmark experiments, respectively (Figure 2-4). The
exploitation of this bias is increased by an order of magnitude in the new ZTA experiment designs.
 To demonstrate the large impact of current differences present in Zr ESADs on the eigenvalues
calculations of benchmark systems, additional calculations were performed using ENDF-8.0 Zr nu-
clear data, but substituting only the JENDL-5.0 Zr ESADs. In Figure 2-5, the differences between
the pink C/E values from the blue (ENDF-8.0) thus represent the contribution of the differing
JENDL-5.0 Zr ESADs from those of ENDF-8.0 to the total C/E discrepancy observed between the
ENDF-8.0 and JENDL-5.0 Zr datasets. In most of these systems, ESADs represent approximately
50% of the discrepancy between the libraries (up to ∼400 PCM), but in certain systems this figure
is closer to 75%.

2.2.1 Compensating Errors
Differences between evaluated nuclear data of a particular isotope are common and arise from various
sources such as differences in nuclear reaction models used or different interpretations of experimen-
tal nuclear data. However, these differences can lead to compensating reactivity affects, or errors, to
produce similar eigenvalue calculations of benchmark systems. These errors are particularly difficult
to untangle especially if little differential nuclear reaction data are available. Figure 2-6 provides
an example of compensating errors between the ENDF-8.0 and JENDL-5.0 Zr evaluations, which
were revealed by propagating Zr cross section differences to system keff calculations of the LCT074
benchmark experiment, case 5, using the methodology described in [24].
 In this instance, differences in the thermal radiative neutron capture cross sections of 91,92 Zr
between the two evaluations considered are observed to partially compensate for the other’s keff
bias. This observation is particularly troublesome given that 91,92 Zr contribute most to the radiative
neutron capture cross section sensitivity of elemental Zr (Figure 2-7) in thermal nuclear systems.
A combination of experiments and measurement techniques can be designed to isolate the effects of
compensating errors following the design methodology demonstrated in the EUCLID experiments
[25].

2.3 New Differential Data and Evaluation

Several differential nuclear data measurements sensitive to Zr have been performed since the most
recent US Zr evaluation for ENDF/B-VII.1 in 2011, some of which are summarized in Table 2-1.
With the compilation of these new experimental nuclear reaction data, the NCSP has sponsored
a new evaluation campaign of the stable Zr isotopes spanning all neutron energy regions [21].
Execution of the proposed Zr integral experiments will both complement and validate the new
differential measurements performed, and ultimately enable nuclear data evaluators to perform
robust validation of the new evaluated Zr datasets in the next release of the ENDF/B nuclear data
library.

 Measurement Type Institution Evaluation Usage Reference
 Neutron Transmission (keV/MeV) RPI Direct Fitting [26]
 Quasi-Differential Scattering (MeV) RPI Validation [27]
 Quasi-Differential Scattering (keV) RPI Validation [28]
 Neutron Transmission (keV) ORNL/JRC-Geel Direct Fitting [29]
  Radiative Neutron Capture (keV) ORNL/JRC-Geel Direct Fitting [29]
 Neutron Transmission (keV) HZBR Validation/Direct Fitting [30]
 D-T Leakage Spectra (MeV) CIAE Validation [31]

 Table 2-1: Zr differential nuclear reaction experiments performed after the ENDF-7.1 Zr evaluations.

2-6 Page Los Alamos National Laboratory

2.3.1 Zirconium Hydride
Thermal neutron scattering law (TSL), or S(α,β), evaluations of neutron scattering from hydrogen
(H) and Zr bonds within the ZrHx lattice have been included in the ENDF/B library since the
ENDF/B-VI (1990s). In which, a S(α,β) ZrHx evaluation performed at LANL [32] was adopted,
and has continued to be retained within the library through the latest release, ENDF-8.1 in 2024.
Recently, new S(α,β) evaluations of H and Zr in δ-phase ZrHx and ϵ-phase ZrHx were performed
[33], and were included in the ENDF-8.1 library. Differential nuclear cross section data are sparse
for both δ-phase and ϵ-phase ZrHx (Figure 2-8), thus these evaluations are most reliant on integral
experiments for validation. Although several ICSBEP experiments posses sensitivity to ZrHx TSLs,
many contain large biases and/or are poorly characterized by modern nuclear data validation stan-
dards. Additionally, no benchmark evaluation contains information specifying the phase(s) of ZrHx
utilized in these experiments. The proposed δ-phase and ϵ-phase ZrHx integral experiments are
essential for validating both the new ENDF-8.1 δ-ZrHx and ϵ-ZrHx S(α,β) evaluations for nuclear
criticality safety.

 (a) (b)

Figure 2-8: (a) ZrHx and (b) ZrH2 total cross sections included in ENDF-8.1 library and available experimental data. Additional
 details regarding these figures can be found in [6].

Los Alamos National Laboratory Page 2-7

3 Experiment Design
This proposed benchmark experiment will validate Zr nuclear data in all energy regions of interest for
nuclear criticality safety applications. Several experimental configurations, ranging from thermal to
fast neutron systems, are required to maximize eigenvalue sensitivity to all pertinent Zr nuclear data.
Table 3-1 lists the energy regions explored in the preliminary experiment design; each energy region
corresponds to at least one unique experimental configuration. Practically, these neutron energy
regions define the bounds of the Zr sensitivity calculations performed. Note that the unresolved
resonance region in several Zr isotopic evaluations begins quite high in incident neutron energy
(>100 keV) and cannot be easily decoupled from the high energy region in a critical experiment.

 Energy Region Corresponding Neutron
 Nomenclature Energy Region
 Thermal Scattering (S(α,β)) En ≤ 2 eV
 Thermal En ≤ 0.625 eV
 Epithermal 0.625eV < En ≤ 100 eV
 Low-Intermediate 100 eV < En ≤ 5 keV
 Intermediate 100 eV < En ≤ 100 keV
 Fast En ≥ 100 keV

 Table 3-1: Neutron energy regions of interest for this experiment design.

3.1 Nuclear Fuel
All ZTA preliminary experiment designs are fueled with highly enriched uranium (HEU) since the
primary application of Zr is cladding LEU fuel rods in thermal nuclear reactors. HEU was selected
over LEU for its superior flexibility and modularity when designing a compact critical system to be
highly sensitive to a test material. Weapons-grade plutonium (Pu) was also considered as the nuclear
fuel for this suite of experiments to leverage the harder PFNS of 239 Pu and increase sensitivity to
high threshold Zr inelastic scattering and (n,2n) reactions (Appendix A). Both the bare metal
HEU Rocky Flat shells and Jemima plates were considered for these experiments [34], but Jemima
plates were ultimately chosen due to the higher Zr sensitivities achieved with a cylindrical system
(Appendix A). LANL first procured the Jemima plates in the 1950s; they have been extensively
characterized [35], and used in numerous criticality experiments, such as the older Zeus experiments
[36] and the new CURIE and TeX-Hf experiments [37][38]. The nominal isotopic composition of the
HEU Jemima plates at NCERC are given in Table 3-2 [36], and the current inventory is provided

                                       236 U           0.33288%
                                       238 U           5.42456%

 The HEU Class Foils available at NCERC were also used to design some of the proposed ZrHx
experimental configurations. This fuel was originally procured to create mock-solution critical ex-
periments for criticality safety applications. There are currently 96 HEU foil pieces of varying sizes
at NCERC; the nominal size of which is 9 in. by 9 in. with a 235 U mass of approximately 65
grams [39]. The average U isotopics of the Class Foils are provided in Table 3-4 [39]. The Class
Foils have been laminated with a polyethylene-based material to prevent oxidation. The lamination
material has been characterized by mass spectroscopy, but uncertainties remain regarding precise
impurity concentrations. Note that oxidation is also visible beneath the lamination on several foils.
These uncertainties, and difficulties quantifying the position of the a foil during an experiment, are
suboptimal for performing a low-uncertainty benchmark experiment, but manageable.

 Table 3-4: Average uranium enrichment of the HEU Class Foils.

3.2 Critical Assembly Machines
The vertical lift critical assembly machines, Comet and Planet, located at NCERC, will be used
for this experimental campaign (Figure 3-1). Criticality is achieved on either vertical lift assembly
machine by remotely raising a portion of the critical mass loaded on the movable platen of the
machine to the remaining critical mass on the top platform. The Comet critical assembly machine
will be used for the Zr metal configurations; whose platform can accommodate up to 20,000 lbs,
and the movable platen up to 2,000 lbs [40]. The lighter ZrHx experiments can be built on either
the Comet or Planet critical assembly machine. Planet can accommodate lower mass experiments
both on its movable platen and atop the platform.

3-2 Page Los Alamos National Laboratory

 Figure 3-1: Comet and Planet Vertical Lift Assembly Machines at NCERC [41].

3.3 Experiment Geometry
3.3.1 Jemima Configurations
For the experiments including Zr metal interstitial plates, 21 in. (or 53.34 cm) outer diameter
(OD) HEU plates will be used to achieve criticality. These full 21 in. diameter HEU plates are
created by nesting 15 in. OD Jemima HEU plates within the 21 in. OD and 15 in. inner diameter
(ID) HEU rings, as show in Figure 3-2. For this preliminary experiment design, only full HEU
plates (no annuluses) were used for all unit cells. In these configurations the Zr metal interstitial
plates and moderator plates (as applicable) were all of equal diameter (21 in.). A large Zr metal
radial and axial reflector, similar to the Zeus copper (Cu) reflector, was implemented to maximize
eigenvalue sensitivity to Zr nuclear data. The precise thicknesses of the reflector, interstitial, and
moderator material for each configuration were optimized for maximal sensitivity to Zr nuclear data
while maintaining a critical system. An example unit cell and a simplified experiment geometry are
provided in Figure 3-3. The neutron moderator was removed from the unit cell in fast configurations.

Los Alamos National Laboratory Page 3-3

Figure 3-2: Nesting of a 15 in. outer diameter HEU plate (HEU1/HEU2) within a 21 in. outer diameter HEU ring (HEU3) to create
 a solid 21 in. HEU fuel plate [36].

Figure 3-3: Simplified ZTA experimental configuration using the HEU Jemima Plates. Note that d1 , d2 , d3 , and d4 correspond to
 dimensions optimized by the K-PSO algorithm during experiment design.

3-4 Page Los Alamos National Laboratory

 Experiments using the HEU Jemima plates and ZrHx (Jemima-ZrHx ) were also designed. In
these systems, both the interstitial Zr metal plates and the radial neutron reflector were removed.
Numerous arrangements of the NCERC Jemima plate inventory were explored in the preliminary
experiment design. Several engineering constraints exist in the manufacturing of ZrHx , most note-
worthy are the dimensional constraints (maximum length in any direction of about 5 in. or 12.7 cm)
present for hydriding the Zr metal within special furnaces. Due to this, many relatively small pieces
of ZrHx will need to be manufactured and arranged together to create the moderator and axial
reflector pieces needed for all proposed Jemima-ZrHx experiments (Figure 3-4). Note that neither
the aluminium (Al) tray, nor polyethylene ridge were modeled during the preliminary experiment
design. Additionally, the ZrHx was modeled as a single solid component.

 Figure 3-4: Aluminum tray with polyethylene ridges holding multiple Zirconium Hydride bricks to form a single moderator
 plate for the Jemima-ZrHx experiments.

 Arranging several small pieces of material together can often be problematic as small air gaps
can create unwanted neutron streaming paths within the experimental geometry. Therefore, the
final experiment design will employ interlocking ZrHx pieces for each experimental configuration to
minimize potential streaming paths; similar to those used in the design of the radial high-density
polyethylene (HDPE) reflectors for the Thermal Epithermal eXperiments (TEX)-HEU experiments,
shown in Figure 3-5.

Los Alamos National Laboratory Page 3-5

 Figure 3-5: Interconnecting HD polyethylene radial neutron reflector pieces used to prevent inadvertent neutron streaming
 paths in case 1 of TEX-HEU (HMM021) [42].

3.3.2 Class Foil Configurations
ZrHx experiments were also designed using the HEU Class Foils (Class Foils-ZrHx ) to create fully
thermalized nuclear systems. These experiment designs were adopted from the HMT032 experiments
[39], which sought to mimic a solution critical experiment by using small HEU masses distributed
within large volumes of polyethylene. Two configurations are considered, one containing a single
HEU foil per unit cell and another containing two HEU foils per unit cell; this resulted in experiments
with differing U:H atom ratios. For this work, all of the polyethylene blocks (nominally 15.4 in. x
15.4 in. square with a thickness of 0.75 in.), inserts (nominally 9.01 in. x 9.01 in. square and 0.26
in. thick), and reflectors (nominally 15.4 in. x 15.4 in. square with a thickness of 1 in.) in the
system were replaced with either delta or epsilon phase ZrHx . Note that the ZrHx reflector and
moderator blocks were reduced from 15.4 in. square to 14.85 in. square to fit on the Comet critical
assembly machine. Examples of the experimental systems are provided in Figure 3-6. Similarly to
the Jeimma-ZrHx experiments, many small bricks of ZrHx need to be properly arranged to form
the moderator and reflector geometries proposed (Figure 3-7).

3-6 Page Los Alamos National Laboratory

 (a) (b)

Figure 3-6: (a) Single HEU foil per unit cell configuration and (b) two HEU foils per unit cell configuration. These figures do not
 reflect the unit cell count of the final preliminary experiment designs.

 Figure 3-7: An example unit cell for the Class Foils-ZrHx experiment design; each consisting of an aluminum tray with
 polyethylene ridges which holds multiple ZrHx bricks to form a single plate.

4 Optimization Methodology
For this experiment campaign to provide robust validation of Zr and ZrHx nuclear data in all energy
regions of interest for nuclear criticality safety applications multiple experiment configurations are
required. Each of these configurations incorporates materials with different nuclear properties in
order to optimize the system for Zr nuclear data sensitivity within the desired window of incident
neutron energy. The optimization methods described in this section were used to identify the
optimal neutron moderator and radial reflector materials (as applicable), corresponding dimensions,
and number of unit cells for each proposed experimental configuration.

4.1 Sensitivity Methodology
The experimental design and optimization strategy performed for this work was built around the
MCNP (Code Version 6.3 [43]) radiation transport code. First, the KCODE feature in MCNP6.3
was utilized to calculate the time-independent eigenvalue quantity, keff , for all systems considered
in this design process. The keff of a multiplying nuclear system is nominally defined as the ratio
of the number of neutrons produced from fission in a generation, Ni , compared to the previous
generation, Ni−1 ; or keff = NNi−1
 i
 . The KSEN feature in MCNP6.3 quantifies how influential certain
factors are to the keff calculation of a multiplying nuclear system [44]. To calculate this sensitivity
to nuclear data specifically, an adjoint neutron flux, arising from a perturbation to some underlying
nuclear data, σx within an energy group, Eg , is calculated using an approximation of the iterated
fission probability method. The change in system keff value determined from this adjoint flux and
the nominal flux, due to the prescribed change in nuclear data, is the sensitivity, or:
 Z
 dkeff σx (E)
 Skeff ,σx (Eg ) = dE (1)
 keff dσx (E)
 Eg

 It is these sensitivity coefficients which provide insight into whether or not the keff of a candidate
experimental configuration will be highly influenced by underlying Zr nuclear data within an energy
region. For this work, an experimental configuration is deemed to be optimized when the system is
both near-critical, and maximizes the desired sensitivity coefficient(s) to Zr nuclear data.

4.2 Particle Swarm Optimization
Preliminary design of this suite of critical experiments required multiple dimensions within the
experimental geometry to be varied in order to obtain optimal configurations for Zr nuclear data
sensitivity. Consider Figure 3-3, where only the thicknesses of the interstitial Zr metal plate (d1 ),
neutron moderator (d2 ), and axial neutron reflector (d3 ), are permitted to change to maximize
the Zr sensitivity of the experiment whilst maintaining criticality. In this case, performing a linear
parameter sweep using ten uniform steps spanning each dimensional range results in 10,000 transport
simulations per material combination. This figure balloons to 100,000 transport simulations if it is
desired to vary a forth dimension, such as radial neutron reflector thickness, (d4 ). This technique
is infeasible for this application since several material combinations must be considered (each with
fine dimensional increments), for a range of potential unit cell counts, in multiple energy regions.
To more efficiently investigate the domain of dimensional combinations for a particular system,
or search space, the Particle Swarm Optimization method (PSO) was used. The PSO method is

Los Alamos National Laboratory Page 4-1

Optimization Methodology

particularly well suited for this application since the method requires no a priori knowledge of the
search domain.
 The particle swarm optimization method was first implemented in critical experiment design by
coupling the PSO algorithm with with MCNP6.2 (K-PSO) through Python [45]. This method was
previously used to design and optimize experiments for molybdenum (Mo) capture cross section
sensitivity in IER 517 (Moby Dick) [41], and for a suite of true intermediate 239 Pu benchmark
experiments in IER 551 (EUROPA) [46]. A substantial refactor of the K-PSO code base was
performed during this preliminary experiment design; which primarily enhanced the robustness of
the code, and expanded its capabilities for use on LANL HPC systems. The PSO technique has the
ability to identify the global optimum point in an n dimensional search domain, Rn , by iteratively
determining the fitness of several candidate solutions (particles) for successive generations of a
population (swarm) to an objective function. For the experimental design performed in this work,
the following piece-wise objective function was maximized.
 (
                                     |Skeff ,σx (Eg ) |         1.00 ≤ keff ≤ 1.02
                         f (xki ) =                                                                   (2)
                                     −1 · |keff − 1|           otherwise

 This function mandates that an experimental configuration (particle) is near-critical, by apply-
ing negative fitness to those which are not, before maximizing a nuclear data sensitivity coefficient.
Thus, dimensional combinations, or particle positions, which are near-critical are always assigned
positive fitnesses. The bounds of allowable system keff were chosen to be up to 2000 PCM su-
percritical as to ensure enough excess reactivity is available in the experiment to reach a critical
configuration despite potentially deficient nuclear data being used during the design process, among
other factors. Several modes of slightly altering system reactivity will be incorporated into the final
experimental designs since retaining a small amount of excess reactivity (a few cents) is desirable
for accurately measuring the reactor period, and thus the experimental keff value. A relatively large
window of criticality also allows for more efficient optimization in a large search space, such as that
explored during this preliminary experiment design phase. The position of each particle, i within
swarm generation k, is defined as a vector, xki ∈ Rn , of the desired optimization dimensions; such
as interstitial plate thickness, moderator thickness, and reflector thickness. For the initial swarm
generation, the locations of each particle are randomly sampled within the specified bounds for each
dimension.

  
 d1
  d2 
                                                 xki =  .                                           (3)
  
  .. 
 dn

 The PSO scheme can more efficiently traverse a search domain because it utilizes information
from previous generations to inform its next steps. Hence after performing the radiation transport of
all dimensional combinations within a swarm, both the best fitness of each particle, pki and holistic
population, gk are retained, and used to update the position of each particle. The movement of
a particle is described by a velocity term, vk+1
 i  , and its position in the forthcoming generation,
xk+1
  i is given below. The velocity term  represents the magnitude of a translation within the search
domain, thus, the units of xkn and vkn are the same.

4-2 Page Los Alamos National Laboratory

 Optimization Methodology

 xk+1
 i = vk+1
 i + xki (4)

Where the velocity term, vk+1
 i is expressed as:

 vk+1
                              i   = ωvki + ϕr1 (pki − xki ) + ρr2 (gk − xki )                       (5)

 Each term in Equation 5 helps describe how the particles will traverse throughout the search
domain. The variables ω, ϕ, and ρ represent unit-less, user-imposed inertial, cognitive, and social
weights, respectively. Intuitively, the inertial weight defines how resistive a particle is to deviating
from its previous trajectory (velocity). Likewise, the cognitive and social weights represent the
motion of particles towards their own best location (pki ), and the best location (gk ) of the swarm,
respectively. The random numbers, r1 and r2 (sampled uniformly (0,1)), are introduced for ad-
ditional nonlinearity. The algorithm performs successive swarm generations using this updating
scheme until the convergence criteria is met. In this case, the convergence criteria was defined as if
the average known best fitness of all particles is within 5% of the best fitness found by the swarm
(Equation 6); where S is the number of particles in the swarm. The location corresponding to the
best fitness of the swarm is then accepted as the global optima of the search domain.
 S
 1X
                                          f (pi ) > 0.95 · f (g)                                    (6)
 S
 i=1

 To illustrate the performance of the K-PSO algorithm a two-dimensional optimization example
is provided. In this demonstration, an uunmoderated stack of 26 unit cells consisting of an HEU
Jemima plate fuel and a Zr metal interstitial plate is to be optimized. The optimization dimensions
include the thickness of the interstitial Zr metal plate and the thickness of the top and bottom axial
Zr metal reflectors. There is no radial reflector in this configuration. The optimization parameter is
the sensitivity of keff to nat Zr radiative neutron capture reactions between 100 keV and 20 MeV. The
particle locations are first initialized and their respective fitness determined in Figure 4-1; where
the blue points represent subcritical configurations, the red points are supercritical configurations,
and the green points are systems which are within the prescribed keff bounds, or near-critical. As
previously described, the algorithm first identifies near-critical configurations (Figure 4-2), before
maximizing the optimization parameter (Figure 4-3). After 20 cycles, this swarm converged on a
global optima of a 16.3 cm axial reflector thickness and a 1.61 cm interstitial plate thickness; with
a Skeff ,σnat Zr from 100 keV to 20 MeV of 0.01582. Figure 4-4 illustrates the convergence of the entire
 γ
swarm (average fitness of all particles within a generation) to the optimal dimensional combination
identified as a function successive generations.

5 Experiment Design Optimization Space

5.1 Optimization Strategy
The K-PSO algorithm was used to optimize all experimental configurations discussed in this re-
port for maximal sensitivity to Zr nuclear data in several energy regions (Table 3-1). Since Zr is a
primarily scattering nuclear material (Figure 5-1), experiments with optimal sensitivity to Zr scat-
tering cross sections are desired. However, optimizing a nuclear system for sensitivity to neutron
scattering, particularly elastic scattering, is difficult as obtaining sensitivity coefficients to neutron
scattering from MCNP KSEN with low uncertainties (≤ 1% to avoid discontinuities in the opti-
mization search domain) is significantly more computationally expensive than obtaining those to
other nuclear reactions, such as capture or fission. Due to this, the optimization strategy chosen
for this preliminary experiment design was to maximize the total sensitivity to 90,91,92,94 Zr neutron
radiative capture (representing 97.2% of elemental Zr) in each experimental configuration. To first
order, it is assumed that due to the low radiative neutron capture cross section of Zr, a nuclear
system which is most sensitive to this parameter will also be highly sensitive to Zr neutron scat-
tering due to the substantial amount of Zr required to achieve high Zr radiative neutron capture
sensitivity. This assumption was verified in the fast neutron energy region; where optimization
sweeps performed maximizing 90 Zr elastic neutron scattering sensitivity resulted in near-identical
optimized dimensional combinations to those of the Zr capture optimization sweep.

5.2 Particle Swarm Characteristics
As mentioned in Section 4.2 the K-PSO algorithm utilizes user-defined weights, or swarm hyper-
parameters, when determining how the particles traverse the search domain as a function of code
iteration. The characteristics supplied to the K-PSO algorithm for this preliminary experiment
design are provided in Table 5-1. Higher inertial, and lower social weights were provided to the K-
PSO code (compared to previous optimization schemes [41]) for this experiment design to decrease
the likelihood of the algorithm falsely converging on a local optima before locating the true global
optima of the search domain.

 S ω ϕ ρ
                                             20    0.6    0.5    0.6

 Table 5-1: Hyper-parameters supplied to the K-PSO algorithm for this work.

5.3 Moderators and Reflectors
A suite of materials with various nuclear properties were considered as both neutron moderators
and reflectors during the optimization of the experimental configurations described in this report.
Pure Zr metal plates (nominal density of 6.56 g/cc), with the same diameter (21 in. or 53.34 cm) as
the nested HEU Jemima plates and rings, were used as an interstitial material to ensure that these
nuclear systems are highly sensitive to Zr cross sections and angular distributions. The thickness
of these plates was optimized by the K-PSO algorithm for all configurations discussed, aside from
those containing ZrHx .

5.3.1 Neutron Moderators
To manipulate the neutron spectrum of each experiment to be maximally sensitive to Zr nuclear
data within the energy regions specified in Table 3-1, several neutron moderating materials were
considered. These moderator plates are right circular cylinders with the same diameter (21 in.
or 53.34 cm) as the nested HEU Jemima plates and rings, and Zr metal interstitial plates. The
thickness of any moderating material within an optimized configuration was determined by the K-
PSO code. All materials considered as neutron moderators for this preliminary experiment design
are provided in Table 5-2, which intentionally contains materials with varying average lethargy gain
per collision to create systems with different neutron spectra. Additionally, the implementation of
absorbing materials (10 B and 6 Li) were explored during this experiment design. The isotopic Li and
B content within the polyethylene moderators containing these additives is provided in Table 5-3.
The use of ZrHx was considered for the Zr metal interstitial configurations, but its usage did not
provide increased sensitivity to non-S(α,β) Zr nuclear data.

5.3.2 Neutron Reflectors
Neutron reflectors allow for a fraction of neutrons to return to a system that would have otherwise
been lost to leakage. Thus, the size/fuel requirement of a system can be decreased while maintaining
both criticality and the desired neutron spectrum. Reflectors can also provide desirable secondary
effects such as moderation and decoupling from room-return and other sources of background radi-
ation. All radial neutron reflector materials considered are listed in Table 5-4. Systems containing
no radial neutron reflector were also considered. For certain reflector materials in Table 5-4 the
thickness of the radial neutron reflector was not optimized since the configuration can be assembled
using materials presently available at NCERC. Two types of reflector geometries were considered
depending on the reflector material: rectangular geometries coincide with a Zeus-style radial re-
flector, as shown in Figure 5-2a; a circular geometry refers to 21 in. (53.34 cm) inner-diameter,
and varying outer-diameter, reflector rings surrounding the stack, as shown in Figure 5-2b. This is
similar to the HDPE rings used as neutron reflectors in the TEX-Hf experiment, although with a
larger diameter. Zr metal was used as the axial neutron reflector, and its thickness optimized by
the K-PSO algorithm, in all designs except those containing ZrHx .

6 Preliminary Experiment Design
This section contains the results from the suite of K-PSO optimizations performed in this preliminary
experiment design. All experimental configurations presented are within 2000 PCM of criticality
(1.00 ≤ keff ≤ 1.02). As mentioned previously, all configurations using the HEU Jemima plates
included the full 21 in. plates during optimization, which can diverge from the available NCERC
inventory. The optimal material combinations and unit cell counts identified for each experiment in
this analysis will be used to determine the final experimental configurations. The ENDF/B-VIII.0
nuclear data library [48] used was to design all new experiments presented.

6.1 Zirconium Metal
Zirconium metal interstitial plates were used along with several moderator-reflector material con-
figurations (Section 5.3) to identify which material combination yielded the optimal experimental
geometry for Zr nuclear data sensitivity within the desired range (Table 3-1). For the thermal,
epithermal, and intermediate energy region configurations the optimized dimensions, 90,91,92,94 Zr
elastic neutron scattering sensitivity, and 90,91,92,94 Zr radiative neutron capture sensitivity, of both
the ten best moderator-reflector material combinations identified, and 25 best overall experiments
designed are reported. A coarse neutron energy spectra, describing the distribution of incident
neutron energies causing fission events, is also provided for each of the ten most sensitive configu-
rations designed in each energy range. The optimization dimensions and aggregate Zr sensitivity
details of the best 100 experiments designed in each energy region are provided in Appendix B.
The dimensional optimization range, unless stated otherwise, for all three-dimensional optimization
sweeps performed with fixed radial reflectors are given in Table 6-1, and for all four-dimensional
(also optimizing radial reflector thickness) sweeps in Table 6-2. The dimensional range considered
for the two-dimensional optimization sweeps performed in the fast region are provided in Section
6.1.4.

6.1.1 Thermal Region
In the thermal region, defined as Zr sensitivity when En ≤ 0.625 eV in this analysis, the hydrogenous
moderators considered (Lucite and HDPE) perform well as both neutron moderators and reflectors.
Beryllium materials are also favorable neutron reflectors in this energy region. The total absolute
sensitivity to 90,91,92,94 Zr radiative neutron capture cross sections of all reflector-moderator material
combinations explored, in the optimal unit cell count of each, are presented in Figure 6-1. Table
6-3 provides the dimensional data for the ten reflector-moderator material combinations yielding
the highest Zr sensitivity values. For each of these designs, a coarse distribution of neutron energies
causing fission events is provided in Figure 6-2. Elemental Zr radiative neutron capture, elastic,
and inelastic neutron scattering sensitivity profiles of the top six reflector-material combinations
identified are presented in Figure 6-3. Lastly, dimensional details of the top twenty-five overall
critical experiments designed for maximal thermal Zr (n,γ) sensitivity are listed in Table 6-4.

6.1.2 Epithermal Region
In the epithermal energy region, defined as Zr sensitivity when 0.625 eV ≤ En ≤ 100 eV for this
analysis, the hydrogenous moderators considered (Lucite and HDPE) similarly perform well as both
as neutron moderators and reflectors. Beryllium materials are again favorable neutron reflectors in
this energy region, along with Teflon® and graphite. However, the use of a Zr metal reflector is
required to achieve maximal sensitivity to Zr (n,γ) cross sections in this energy region. The total
absolute sensitivity to 90,91,92,94 Zr radiative neutron capture cross sections of all reflector-moderator
material combinations explored, in the optimal unit cell count of each, are presented in Figure 6-4.
Note that for this optimization sweep, the maximum Zr metal interstitial thickness was increased
from 5.0 cm to 7.5 cm. Table 6-5 provides the dimensional data for the ten reflector-moderator
material combinations yielding the highest Zr (n,γ) sensitivity values; it is clear that the Zr reflector
is essential to maximize neutron scattering cross section sensitivities. Experiments incorporating
the Zr reflector have nearly an order of magnitude more sensitivity to Zr elastic neutron scattering
cross sections compared to most other reflectors amidst smaller gains in Zr (n,γ) sensitivity. For
each design highlighted in Table 6-5, a coarse distribution of neutron energies causing fission events
is provided in Figure 6-5. Elemental Zr radiative neutron capture, elastic, and inelastic neutron
scattering sensitivity profiles of the top five reflector-material combinations identified are presented
in Figure 6-6. Lastly, dimensional details of the top twenty-five overall critical experiments designed
for maximal epithermal Zr (n,γ) sensitivity are listed in Table 6-6

6.1.3 Intermediate Region

The intermediate neutron energy region, defined as Zr sensitivity when 100 eV ≤ En ≤ 100 keV for
this analysis, encompasses the majority of the resolved resonance regions of all the stable Zr isotopes.
Maximum sensitivity to Zr (n,γ) cross section sensitivity in this energy region was achieved using
Be materials (Be oxide (BeO)/Be metal) or Teflon® ((C2 F4 )n ) neutron moderators, along with
a Zr metal reflector. The implementation of Cu, BeO, or Teflon® neutron reflectors resulted in
over a 20% loss in Zr (n,γ) sensitivity for this energy region compared to that achieved with a Zr
reflector. The total absolute sensitivity to 90,91,92,94 Zr radiative neutron capture cross sections of
all reflector-moderator material combinations explored, in the optimal unit cell count of each, are
presented in Figure 6-7. Table 6-7 provides the dimensional data for the ten reflector-moderator
material combinations yielding the highest Zr (n,γ) sensitivity values. Similarly to the epithermal
design results, it is clear that the Zr reflector is essential to maximize neutron scattering cross section

6.1.4 Fast Region
The fast neutron energy region, defined as Zr sensitivity when En ≥ 100 keV for this analysis,
encompasses the high end of the resolved resonance regions, the unresolved resonance regions, and
high energy regions of all the stable Zr isotopes. The critical experiments designed for optimal
sensitivity to Zr nuclear data in the high energy region did not include a neutron moderator. An
example of a unit cell for these configurations is provided in Figure 6-10, and optimization space in
Table 6-9.

 Figure 6-10: Unit cell for the fast energy region experimental configurations.

 Dimension Min. [cm] Max. [cm] Increment [cm]
                    d1 (Interstitial)               0.0               5.0                  0.01
                  d2 (Axial Reflector)              0.0              25.0                  0.01

 Table 6-9: Optimization search domain for the fast configurations

Los Alamos National Laboratory Page 6-13

 In effort to preserve a fast neutron spectrum, only Zr metal, and other low lethargy gain per
collision neutron reflector materials such as Cu and depleted uranium (DU) were considered as radial
neutron reflectors. An experiment was also designed without including a radial neutron reflector
to maximize sensitivity to Zr angular distributions. The total absolute sensitivity to 90 Zr elastic
neutron scattering cross sections in experiments, with the optimal unit cell count, employing the
aforementioned neutron reflectors in the high energy region are presented in Figure 6-11. Table
6-10 provides dimensional, elemental Zr elastic scattering cross section sensitivity, and elemental Zr
ESAD sensitivity data for these critical experiment designs. Additionally, a coarse distribution of
neutron energies causing fission events for each design is provided in Figure 6-12. Finally, sensitivity
profiles of several nuclear reactions of interest (elastic and inelastic neutron scattering, (n,2n), and
ESADs) are provided in Figure 6-13.

6.2 Zirconium Hydride
Critical experiments sensitive to the ZrHx S(α,β) TSLs were designed using two forms of HEU fuel
available at NCERC. The first, or high HEU mass configuration type, utilized the HEU Jemima
plates, similar to the previous section. The second, or low HEU mass configuration type, utilized the
HEU Class Foils. For each configuration type, experiments were designed with both delta (δ) and
epsilon (ϵ) phases of ZrHx . The ZrHx thermal scattering law evaluation in the ENDF-8.0 evaluated
nuclear data library was used to design and obtain nuclear data sensitivity profiles to ZrHx TSLs
in all experiments presented.

6.2.1 Jemima Configurations
To design the high HEU mass Jemima-ZrHx experiments, the K-PSO algorithm was used. How-
ever, instead of optimizing the experiments to be maximally sensitivity to the ZrHx directly, the
configurations were optimized for thermal fission fraction. This optimization strategy was chosen
since the computational resources required to iteratively determine low uncertainty (< 1%) sensi-
tivity coefficients to either H or Zr in ZrHx TSLs far exceed those currently available. Additionally,
maximizing thermal fission events also maximizes the thermal neutron flux of the system, and thus
the number of possible ZrHx S(α,β) thermal scattering reactions. The optimization space explored
by the K-PSO algorithm in these two-dimensional optimization sweeps is provided in Table 6-11.

 Dimension Min. [cm] Max. [cm] Increment [cm]
                 d1 (Moderator)             0.01              5.0                0.005
               d2 (Axial Reflector)          0.5             10.0                0.05

 Table 6-11: Optimization search domain for the Jemima-ZrHx optimization sweeps.

 Several Jemima HEU fuel arrangements were considered when designing Jemima-ZrHx experi-
ments. These arrangements being full 21 in. OD plates (similar to the Zr metal experiment designs),
15 in OD. plates containing no annulus (center hole) (15/0), a 6 in. annulus (15/6), and finally a 10
in. annulus (15/10). In all designs, ZrHx was used as both the neutron moderator and axial reflec-
tor. No cases utilized a radial neutron reflector. Installation of the 21 in. Jemima plates was too
reactive, needing only two plates to achieve criticality in certain arrangements, whilst over twenty
15/10 HEU fuel units were needed to reach criticality in the absence of a radial neutron reflector.
The 15/6 Jemima plates (∼5.45 kg HEU each) were thus selected for the preliminary experiment
design. The omission of a radial ZrHx neutron reflector from these configurations is partially due to
the fact that maximally-thermal radially-reflected ZrHx experiments were designed using the HEU
Class Foils for this experiment campaign (Section 6.2.2). But more importantly, to increase and
isolate sensitivity to the average thermal neutron scattering angle (µ̄) and accompanying thermal
scattering angular distributions, following the same methodology to the unreflected fast Zr metal
configuration discussed (Section 7.1.5). Both the δ-ZrH1.6 and ϵ-ZrH2 , experiments were optimized
for thermal neutron spectra for a variety of fuel units. The optimized dimensions, coarse neutron
spectrum, and sensitivity profiles for the δ-ZrH1.6 -Jemima experiment designs are presented in Ta-
ble 6-12, Figure 6-14, Figure 6-15, respectively. Likewise the optimized dimensions, coarse neutron
spectrum, and sensitivity profiles for the ϵ-ZrH2 -Jemima experiment designs are presented in Table
6-13, Figure 6-16, and Figure 6-17, respectively.

6.2.2 Class Foil Configurations
As mentioned in Section 3.3.2, the design of the low HEU mass Class Foil-ZrHx experiments was
adopted from the HMT032 experiments [39]. Two distinct configurations were considered for each
phase of ZrHx . The first included only a single HEU foil (∼ 69.4g HEU) per unit cell (Case 1),
while the second implemented two HEU foils (∼ 138.8g HEU) per unit (Case 2). No dimensional
optimization was performed for these experiments. Given that these experiments originally sought
to mimic solution critical experiments, the resultant neutron spectra is fully thermalized, far softer
than those of the ZrHx -Jemima experiments. Details of the four experiments designed are provided
in Table 6-14, and coarse neutron spectra in Figure 6-18. All experiments utilized four 1 in. (2.54
cm) ZrHx top and bottom axial reflector blocks of the appropriate phase. The sensitivity profiles of
all ZrHx TSLs reactions in each of the Class Foil-ZrHx experiments designed are shown in Figure
6-19, where it is clear that the experiment has higher sensitivity to H thermal scattering compared
to Zr thermal scattering in ZrHx .

7 Discussion
In this section, results from the preliminary experiment design performed are interpreted and the
most optimal preliminary configurations are selected. Zr and ZrHx nuclear data sensitivities of
the most optimal experiments designed are compared to those in available ICSBEP benchmark
experiments. Engineering and inventory constraints, such as the maximum allowable stack height
on Comet for forklift operations and Comet/Planet platen weight limits were loosely considered
during the preliminary design; as the primary objective of this analysis was to curate the optimal
experiments for Zr nuclear data sensitivity from a neutronic perspective. Therefore, some of the
optimal experiments selected will require modifications to be constructed at NCERC; the refinement
of these specific systems will be included in the CED-2, as applicable.

7.1 Zirconium Metal
New experiments have been designed to be maximally sensitive to Zr cross sections in the ther-
mal, epithermal, intermediate, and fast neutron energy regimes (Table 3-1); each of which employ
different neutron moderators and radial neutron reflector materials to achieve the desired neutron
spectrum within the Zr. It is important to discuss the advantages and disadvantages of most the
optimal materials selected for each configuration by the K-PSO algorithm, in addition to the com-
parative performance, or Zr sensitivity, of each to existing ICSBEP benchmark experiments.

7.1.1 Thermal Region
In the thermal neutron energy region (En ≤ 0.625 eV) the hydrogenous neutron moderator and re-
flector materials performed well due to the high average lethargy gain per collision of 1 H. However,
the K-PSO algorithm identified that the highest sensitivity to Zr thermal radiative neutron capture
cross sections was achieved by utilizing a thick (25 cm) BeO radial reflector and a Teflon® mod-
erator (Table 6-3). Utilizing the same thick BeO reflector with a graphite moderator also yielded
configurations highly sensitive to Zr thermal neutron capture data (Table 6-4). The implementation
of a 25 cm BeO reflector is unfavorable for this experiment for the following reasons:

  1. Procurement of this quantity of BeO is difficult.

  2. The implementation of BeO would introduce additional working hazards and/or encapsulation
 requirements for little gain in thermal Zr (n,γ) sensitivity.

  3. A BeO radial neutron reflector coupled with non-hydrogenous neutron moderators (Teflon®
 or graphite) results in a majority intermediate nuclear system (Figure 6-2).

 For these reasons, it is more desirable to consider the next most optimal material selection for
the Zr thermal experiment: high-density polyethylene. HDPE was shown to be a favorable neutron
moderator and radial reflector for this energy region, yielding configurations highly sensitive to
Zr thermal (n,γ) cross section data both when including a radial neutron reflector and not. The
reduction in Zr thermal radiative neutron capture sensitivity compared to that of the optimal BeO-
reflected configuration is acceptable, being only ∼3%, while enabling both a 46% increase in thermal
Zr elastic neutron scattering cross section sensitivity (Table 6-3), and creating a majority thermal
nuclear system (Figure 6-2). The Zr elastic neutron scattering cross section sensitivity enabled in
this configuration compared to ICSBEP benchmark experiments is shown in Figure 7-1, and likewise

Los Alamos National Laboratory Page 7-1

the Zr radiative neutron capture sensitivity in Figure 7-2. The magnitude of the Zr thermal capture
cross section sensitivity of the newly designed experiments are comparable to current ICSBEP
benchmarks such as LCT074 case 5 and ICT003 case 2. However, the Zr elastic neutron scattering
sensitivities achieved in these new experiments surpass those available in current benchmarks by
an order of magnitude. The deviation in system eigenvalue, keff , calculations using different Zr
evaluations observed both in the new experiments and ICSBEP benchmarks are presented in Table
7-1; additional ∆keff information for Zr isotopic reaction cross section contributions are provided in
Appendix C. Most notably, the HDPE-reflected experiment better exploits the compensating errors
observed in the thermal radiative neutron capture cross sections of 91,92 Zr between the ENDF-8.0
and JENDL-5.0 evaluations than both the ICT003 and LCT074 benchmark experiments (Figure
7-3). Finally, this experiment would be the first HEU-Zr thermal benchmark, and would enable the
highest sensitivities to Zr thermal cross section data without convolution from ZrHx TSLs (ICT003).

7.1.2 Epithermal Region
In the epithermal neutron energy region (0.625 eV ≤ En ≤ 100 eV) the hydrogenous neutron mod-
erator materials, HDPE and Lucite, also perform well due to the high average lethargy gain per
collision of 1 H. Unlike the thermal configurations, the desired neutron spectrum for maximum sen-
sitivity to the epithermal region of the Zr radiative neutron capture cross section is slightly harder,
as seen in Figure 6-5. To achieve the desired neutron spectrum and Zr nuclear data sensitivities, a
large (16.35 cm or 6.44 in.) Zr metal radial neutron reflector was implemented in the experiment
design. The use of a Zr radial reflector yielded systems not only with maximal Zr (n,γ) sensitivity,
but also with an order of magnitude more sensitivity to epithermal Zr elastic neutron scattering
cross sections (Table 6-5). HDPE was selected as the neutron moderator for the final preliminary
experiment design for consistency with the thermal configuration. Zr nuclear data sensitivity pro-
files for radiative neutron capture and elastic neutron scattering between ICSBEP benchmarks and
the optimal epithermal ZTA experiment designs are presented in Figure 7-4a and 7-4b, respectively.
The newly designed ZTA experiments enable double the Zr (n,γ) and (n,elastic) cross section sen-
sitivity achieved in available ICSBEP benchmarks. This finding is particularly encouraging given
that there is very little isotopically-enriched differential Zr radiative neutron capture cross section
data available to nuclear data evaluators in this energy region.
 The deviation in system eigenvalue, keff , calculations using different Zr evaluations observed
both in the new ZTA epithermal experiments and ICSBEP benchmarks are presented in Table
7-2; additional ∆keff information for Zr isotopic reaction cross section contributions are provided
in Appendix C. This experiment further exploits the compensating errors observed in the thermal
radiative neutron capture cross sections of 91,92 Zr between the ENDF-8.0 and JENDL-5.0 evaluations
up into the epithermal region (Figure 7-5). Note that the K-PSO algorithm was permitted to
utilize thicker (7.5 cm) Zr metal interstitial plates to observe the sensitivity and reactivity effects of
increasing interstitial thickness. Due to the low thermal neutron capture cross section of Zr, adding
more interstitial Zr inserts little negative reactivity to the system.

7.1.3 Intermediate Region

In the intermediate neutron energy region (100 eV ≤ En ≤ 100 keV) higher mass neutron moderator
materials are preferred (Be, F, O) over hydrogenous neutron moderator materials due to the lower
average lethargy gain of these materials per collision (Figure 6-7). Specifically, selecting a Be
metal, BeO, or Teflon® neutron moderator, and implementing a large Zr metal radial neutron
reflector (16.35 cm or 6.44 in.) enabled maximal sensitivity to both Zr (n,γ) and (n,elastic) cross
sections in the intermediate energy region (Figure 6-7). Similar to the epithermal configurations,
implementation of a Zr metal reflector lead to an increase in Zr (n,elastic) cross section sensitivity
by a factor of 3.5 over all other radial neutron reflector materials (Table 6-7), further demonstrating
its importance. Similarly, Zr (n,γ) cross section sensitivity immediately decreases by 20% when
substituting the Zr radial reflector for any other material considered (Table 6-7).
 Zr nuclear data sensitivity profiles for neutron capture and elastic neutron scattering between
ICSBEP benchmarks and the most sensitive intermediate ZTA experiment designs are presented
in Figures 7-6 and 7-7a, respectively. Both the Zr (n,γ) and (n,elastic) cross section sensitivity
achieved in the newly designed experiments surpasses those of available ICSBEP benchmarks by
over an order of magnitude. In addition, the positive eigenvalue bias introduced by JEFF-3.3 90 Zr
(Figure 2-3a) is much better exploited by this experiment relative those in the ICSBEP (Figure
7-8). The deviation in system eigenvalue, keff , calculations using different Zr evaluations observed
both in the new experiments and ICSBEP benchmarks are presented in Table 7-3; additional ∆keff
information for Zr isotopic reaction cross section contributions are provided in Appendix C. This
configuration is particularly important as the intermediate energy region corresponds to the resolved

7-6 Page Los Alamos National Laboratory

resonance region of all Zr isotopes, enabling evaluators to validate resonances parameters with low-
uncertainty integral nuclear data.

7.1.4 Low-Intermediate Region
The intermediate energy region, defined as Zr sensitivity when 100 eV ≤ En ≤ 100 keV in this analy-
sis, includes the major resonances of the stable Zr isotopes, particularly those of 91,96 Zr between 100
eV and 5 keV (Figure 7-9), and possesses the least integral validation data (Figure 2-1). These reso-
nances are some of the largest contributors to the radiative neutron capture sensitivity of elemental
Zr in thermal nuclear systems (Figure 2-7). Although these resonances have been studied with
differential measurements [51][52], validation of these resonance parameters via a low-uncertainty
critical experiments is currently unavailable. Due to this, it is desirable to isolate and maximize sen-
sitivity to this important region of the Zr (n,γ) cross section with an additional integral experiment.
Using an experiment geometry consistent with the optimal intermediate ZTA experiment design,
the K-PSO algorithm was used (with hyper-parameters (Table 5-1) and optimization dimensions
(Table 6-1)) to optimize elemental Zr(n,γ) sensitivity in this important energy region. Note that to
create a softer neutron spectrum the maximum moderator optimization dimension was increased to

Los Alamos National Laboratory Page 7-9

7.5 cm. The resultant configurations implement the same large Zr metal radial neutron reflector as
the current optimal ZTA epithermal, intermediate, and reflected-fast experiments (Section 6.1).

 Similarly to the optimal intermediate experiment design (Section 6.1.3), the optimal low inter-
mediate experiment requires a neutron moderator with lower average lethargy gain per collision

 Another optimization sweep was performed to design a high-intermediate critical experiment (1
keV - 100 keV). However, the Zr nuclear data sensitivity gains over the previously designed interme-
diate configuration were minimal (<10%). Therefore, the current ZTA intermediate configuration
is considered to be sufficient in this energy region.

7.1.5 Fast Region
In the fast energy region (En ≥ 100 keV) no neutron moderators were implemented to preserve the
high energy spectrum of fission neutrons. Several high mass (low average lethargy gain per collision)
radial neutron reflector materials were considered for this configuration. Similarly to the epithermal,
low-intermediate, and intermediate experiments, the implementation of a large (16.35 cm or 6.44 in.)
Zr metal radial reflector enabled maximal sensitivity to Zr nuclear data (Figure 6-11). Comparisons
of Zr nuclear data sensitivity profiles from available ICSBEP benchmarks and the optimal ZTA fast
experiments designed are presented in Figures 7-13 and 7-15. The deviation in system eigenvalue,
keff , calculations using different Zr evaluations observed both in the new experiments and ICSBEP
benchmarks are presented in Table 7-7; additional ∆keff information for Zr isotopic reaction cross
section contributions are provided in Appendix C. It is observed that greater sensitivity to fast Zr
nuclear reaction data is present in available thermal benchmark experiments (ICT003 and UCT001)
as opposed to fast spectrum experiments containing Zr (HMF061, MMF011, and PMF033). Thus,
it is clear that fast spectrum benchmark experiments highly sensitive to Zr nuclear data are absent
from the ICSBEP handbook. Fast Zr sensitivities enabled by the proposed experiments far exceed

those currently available in the aforementioned ICSBEP benchmarks. Most notably, sensitivity to
Zr elastic scattering cross sections is over triple that of those presently available, which exploits
the the positive eigenvalue bias of JEFF-3.3 relative to ENDF/B-8.0 due to differences in Zr elastic
scattering cross sections and angular distribution (Figure 2-3) to a much greater degree (Figure
7-14). Additionally, fast Zr neutron radiative capture cross section sensitivity is increased by an
order of magnitude, and Zr inelastic neutron scattering cross section sensitivity by 2-10x below 2
MeV. Zr (n,2n) sensitivities were also observed to increase by a factor of five.
 The fast configurations of ZTA are particularly important to nuclear data evaluators due to
the lack of fast differential and integral Zr nuclear data available to constrain and validate new Zr
evaluations. Unlike the resonance regions, fast incident neutron evaluations must consider more
reaction channels and neutron-nucleus interaction mechanisms whilst generally having few differ-
ential nuclear reaction data available. Constraining ESADs for example, is often challenging since
isotopically enriched double-differential cross section data are required but are seldom available (as
is the case for multiple Zr isotopes). Fast critical experiments can be utilized to partially fill this
data gap by manipulating neutron leakage (i.e. removing the radial neutron reflector). In Figure
7-15a, neutron elastic scattering law (angular distribution) sensitivity profiles are shown for current
ICSBEP benchmark experiments and the proposed ZTA experiments. The radially reflected fast
ZTA experiment is observed to increase Zr ESAD sensitivity by an order of magnitude at backward
scattering angles (µ < -0.75) but possess little sensitivity to forward neutron scattering angles (µ
> 0.75). In contrast, the unreflected fast ZTA experiment doubles presently available sensitivities
to Zr forward neutron scattering angles and enables even higher sensitivity at all backward neutron

scattering angles. Execution of both radially reflected and unreflected Zr fast critical experiments
aids in both decoupling eigenvalue sensitivity to forward and backward elastic neutron scattering
sensitivities, and partially isolating sensitivities to ESADs from that of their corresponding integral
cross sections (Figure 7-15c). Thus enabling nuclear data evaluators to, for the first time, constrain
new Zr high energy region evaluations to highly sensitive, low-uncertainty critical benchmark ex-
periments. The design and analysis of these systems further demonstrates both the necessity of
critical benchmark experiments for nuclear data validation, and the ability to manipulate critical
experiments to address specific nuclear data needs.

7.2 Zirconium Hydride
Two types of ZrHx benchmark experiments were produced in this preliminary experimental design.
The first design being a high HEU mass platform utilizing the Jemima plates, and the second being a
low HEU mass configuration using the Class Foils. Critical configurations of both experiment types
were designed using both δ-phase and ϵ-phase ZrHx . Experiment design began with the Jemima-
ZrHx platform using the full 21" HEU Jemima plates. However, the exceptional moderating power
of ZrHx allowed for as few as two HEU units to achieve criticality in certain arrangements. Since
this configuration is not safe to build, the fuel was changed to the 15 in. OD and 6 in. ID HEU
Jemima plates, which reduced the HEU mass per unit cell from ∼12.6 kg to ∼5.45 kg. Given this,
taller stacks with more unit cells were required to achieve criticality, and were observed to increase
ZrHx TSL sensitivities. The optimal experiment designs identified for both δ-phase ZrH1.6 and
ϵ-phase ZrH2 included twelve unit cells, each of which contains ZrHx moderator plates and an axial
ZrHx reflector of the appropriate phase. In the final design phase, the effect of reintroducing a
radial neutron reflector with the 15/10 HEU plates or 21/15 HEU rings may be considered.
 The second type of critical benchmark experiment designed for ZrHx TSL validation, using the
HEU Class Foils (∼65 g HEU per foil), created a fully thermalized, low HEU mass experiment -
similar to a solution critical experiment due to the high H:U ratio (Table 7-8). These experiments
possess a much softer neutron spectrum compared to the Jemima-ZrHx experiments (Figure 7-16).
Configurations using unit cells containing both a single HEU foil and two HEU foils were designed
to observe the effect of ZrHx sensitivities as a function of U:H atomic ratio between experiments.
A total of four critical configurations were designed using the HEU Class Foils, each implementing
either δ-phase ZrHx and ϵ-phase ZrHx , for both single and double HEU foil unit cells (Table 6-14).
Between the two configuration types, experiments including two HEU foils per unit cell of both

Figure 7-17: Differences in ZrHx TSL sensitivity profiles between the Jemima-ϵ-ZrHx and Class Foil-ϵ-ZrHx experiment designs.

7-18 Page Los Alamos National Laboratory

and the Jemima-ZrHx do not. For example, thermal scattering from both H and Zr in ZrHx insert
negative reactivity in the Jemima-ZrHx experiments while generally inserting positive reactivity
into the Class Foil-ZrHx experiments. This dichotomy helps decouple sensitivities between ther-
mal scattering cross sections and angular distributions, similarly to fast neutron systems (Section
7.1.5). The sensitivity profiles for both the optimal ϵ-ZrH2 Jemima and Class Foil experiments are
shown in Figure 7-17. Sensitivity profiles for the corresponding δ-ZrH1.6 experiments are provided
in Appendix D. Since ZrHx TSLs are manipulating the reactivity of each experiment differently, the
potential for experimental correlations is decreased, thus enabling more robust validation of ZrHx
TSL data.
 Both the optimal Jemima-ZrHx and Class Foil-ZrHx experiments designed enable greater sen-
sitivities to ZrHx TSL data than those currently available in ICSBEP benchmark experiments.
Sensitivity profiles of ZrHx TSL data in both the new ϵ-ZrH2 experiment designs and current ICS-
BEP benchmarks are presented in Figure 7-18. Those for the corresponding δ-ZrH1.6 experiments
are provided in Appendix D. Differences in C/E or keff values of the ICSBEP benchmarks and ZTA
experiments due to differences in ZrHx TSL evaluated nuclear data between the ENDF/B-VIII.0,
JEFF-3.3, and JENDL-5.0 libraries are reported in Table 7-9. Note that certain ICSBEP experi-
ments which contain ZrHx were excluded from this analysis (ICT013, HCT007, and HCM004) due
to either large unresolved eigenvalue biases and/or modeling issues. Also note that the JEFF-3.3
library only contains H in ZrHx TSL data, and the JENDL-5.0 library was adopted from ENDF-8.0,
but evaluated on a finer energy grid (to better resolve H in ZrHx cross section oscillations at higher
(>0.1 eV) incident energies [53]).

 Table 7-9: Differences between calculated system keff values of ZTA ZrHx experiment designs and C/E (calculation over
experiment) values from ICSBEP benchmarks using ENDF-8.0, JEFF-3.3. and JENDL-5.0 nuclear data libraries.∆keff or ∆(C/E)
 values are given in PCM, and all keff statistical uncertainties (1σ) are below 15 PCM.

 Execution, characterization, and documentation of the new ZrHx critical experiments designed
will enable experimental validation to the new δ-phase and ϵ-phase ZrHx evaluations included in
the ENDF-8.1 nuclear data library. Currently, the phase of ZrHx used in all ICSBEP benchmarks is
unspecified which creates ambiguity as to which TSL evaluation is appropriate to validate with each
experiment. Certain experiments additionally used U-ZrHx which introduces molecular bonding ef-
fects independent of those present in the ZrHx matrix, further convoluting the ZrHx TSL validation.
Given the collective interest in performing additional ZrHx benchmark experiments between LANL
and ASNR it may be most appropriate to a separate IER specifically for ZrHx critical experiments.
This is due to both the expected costs to procure the ZrHx needed to perform these experiments
alongside the interest in expanding the experimental campaign to include additional configurations
using U-ZrHx fuels.
